========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, mostly I'm here because I don't sleep right anymore. I just lie there a long time in the dark. It reminds me of back when I was working on the cars, you know, my buddy Earl and I would be up half the night trying to get an engine to turn over. Earl always had this old radio playing, static mostly... anyway, now I finally drift off and then I wake up from bad dreams or just little noises outside, like a dog barking three streets over, and I can't seem to settle back down. I tried drinking a glass of warm milk, my mother used to swear by that when I was a boy, but it just gave me heartburn.

Concentrate? No, I wouldn't say that. My mind is still pretty sharp, and I don't really get tired during the day, neither. I mostly just sit out on the porch or watch the television. 

Course, the TV's been acting up lately. The picture gets all fuzzy on the local channel. Reminds me of this big heavy Zenith set my wife and I bought back in, oh, must've been '78. Cost me a week's pay, and we still had to fiddle with the rabbit ears for an hour just to watch the evening news. We loved Walter Cronkite. They just don't make newsmen like him anymore, do they? Now everybody on the screen is just yelling at each other about something, makes my head spin just listening to 'em. 

But, uh... what were we... oh, no, I'm not fatigued or nothing like that. Just regular, I guess.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, the dreams are mostly about when it happened. When it all came down. I wake up and for a second I think I'm still stuck. But it happens when I'm awake, too. Sometimes if I just hear a piece of metal scrape, or if I'm out walking and see a ladder leaning in somebody's yard, the whole thing just jumps back in my head. It's like I'm right back under there again waiting for help.

Course, ladders remind me of this old aluminum extension ladder I used to have. Bought it at the Sears and Roebuck over in the valley. Must have been the mid-eighties. Sears had a real nice hardware department back then. You could walk in and smell the rubber tires and the machine oil. I bought a Craftsman drill the same day. Heavy thing, solid metal casing. Not like the cheap plastic junk they sell today. My grandson ordered some tools off the computer last month, and I told him he was just throwing his money away on that plastic. He didn't listen, though. Nobody listens to the old guys anymore. 

But, uh... what was I saying? Right, the dreams. Yeah, they just put me right back there.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I suppose I do. I just... I keep finding reasons not to go down to the senior center these days. And I sure don't go out to work around the garage much anymore, especially not near the tools or that spot where I used to keep that ladder. Just don't want to be around it. 

Course, the garage is a mess anyway. I got boxes of old oil filters and spark plugs out there from when I used to fix up that old Ford I had. Beautiful car, that was. A '64 Fairlane. Cherry red. I used to wash it every Sunday morning before church. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he had a Chevrolet, and he was always trying to race me down to the stop sign. Henderson was a funny guy, always wore these bright yellow suspenders. Said they kept his pants up better than a belt because he didn't have no waist. His wife made the best peach cobbler, though. She'd bring a whole pan over on the Fourth of July, fresh peaches right off the tree in their backyard. You just can't buy peaches like that at the supermarket anymore. They pick 'em too green now, and they taste like wet cardboard by the time they get to the produce aisle. It's a real shame, if you ask me. 

But, uh... what was it you asked me? Oh, right, avoiding things. Yeah, I just find excuses not to go out to the garage or the center. Just easier to stay on the porch.

Cost me a week's pay, and we still had to fiddle with the rabbit ears for an hour just to watch the evening news. We loved Walter Cronkite. They just don't make newsmen like him anymore, do they? Now everybody on the screen is just yelling at each other about something, makes my head spin just listening to 'em. \n\nBut, uh... what were we... oh, no, I'm not fatigued or nothing like that. Just regular, I guess.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned waking up from bad dreams, can you tell me more about those dreams?\n\nPatient: Well, the dreams are mostly about when it happened. When it all came down. I wake up and for a second I think I'm still stuck. But it happens when I'm awake, too. Sometimes if I just hear a piece of metal scrape, or if I'm out walking and see a ladder leaning in somebody's yard, the whole thing just jumps back in my head. It's like I'm right back under there again waiting for help.\n\nCourse, ladders remind me of this old aluminum extension ladder I used to have. Bought it at the Sears and Roebuck over in the valley. Must have been the mid-eighties. Sears had a real nice hardware department back then. You could walk in and smell the rubber tires and the machine oil. I bought a Craftsman drill the same day. Heavy thing, solid metal casing. Not like the cheap plastic junk they sell today. My grandson ordered some tools off the computer last month, and I told him he was just throwing his money away on that plastic. He didn't listen, though. Nobody listens to the old guys anymore. \n\nBut, uh... what was I saying? Right, the dreams. Yeah, they just put me right back there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities because they remind you of what happened?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do. I just... I keep finding reasons not to go down to the senior center these days. And I sure don't go out to work around the garage much anymore, especially not near the tools or that spot where I used to keep that ladder. Just don't want to be around it. \n\nCourse, the garage is a mess anyway. I got boxes of old oil filters and spark plugs out there from when I used to fix up that old Ford I had. Beautiful car, that was. A '64 Fairlane. Cherry red. I used to wash it every Sunday morning before church. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he had a Chevrolet, and he was always trying to race me down to the stop sign. Henderson was a funny guy, always wore these bright yellow suspenders. Said they kept his pants up better than a belt because he didn't have no waist. His wife made the best peach cobbler, though. She'd bring a whole pan over on the Fourth of July, fresh peaches right off the tree in their backyard. You just can't buy peaches like that at the supermarket anymore. They pick 'em too green now, and they taste like wet cardboard by the time they get to the produce aisle. It's a real shame, if you ask me. \n\nBut, uh... what was it you asked me? Oh, right, avoiding things. Yeah, I just find excuses not to go out to the garage or the center. Just easier to stay on the porch.\n\n[Last Question]\n{\"intent\": \"determine the presence of hyperarousal\", \"question\": \"Do you often feel \"on edge\" or easily startled?\"}\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 74 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Exaggerated_Startle_Response\nmanifestation: \"If a door bangs or someone comes up behind me, I jump hard and my chest keeps thumping for a while even after I know it's nothing.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, I guess I do. If a door bangs or someone comes up behind me, I jump hard. My chest just keeps thumping for a while, even after I know it's nothing. 

Course, doors banging reminds me of the screen door we had on the old house back in, oh, must have been '68. The spring on that thing was so tight, it would slam shut like a gunshot every time one of the kids ran out to the yard. I tried to fix it once with some wire I had laying around, but it just made it worse. My oldest boy, Tommy, he used to let it slam on purpose just to get a rise out of the dog. We had this old hound named Buster. Best hunting dog I ever saw, but he was terrified of thunderstorms. If he even smelled rain coming, he'd crawl right under the porch and wouldn't come out for two days. Tommy loved that dog, though. Fed him scraps from the table when his mother wasn't looking. She always made this incredible meatloaf on Tuesdays. Had a special glaze she put on top with brown sugar and ketchup. I tried to make it myself a few years back, but it just didn't taste the same. I think they changed the ketchup recipe. Everything tastes different now, all high-fructose corn syrup and preservatives. 

But, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, being on edge. Yeah, I jump pretty easy these days. My heart just races.

Well, I try to. I mean, like I said before, I keep finding reasons not to go down to the senior center, and I sure don't go out to work around the garage much anymore. Especially not near the tools or that spot where I used to keep the ladder. If I'm not around it, maybe I won't think about it so much. 

Course, thinking about the garage makes me think of my old neighbor, Mr. Henderson. I mentioned him earlier, the one with the yellow suspenders. He actually helped me build some shelves out there back in... oh, must have been '92. We used this heavy oak plywood. Real sturdy stuff. Not like that particle board garbage they sell down at the big box stores now. You put a heavy toolbox on that particle board and it just bows right in the middle. Henderson had this old circular saw that weighed about twenty pounds. Sounded like an airplane taking off when you fired it up. He wouldn't let anyone else use it. Said you had to know its "quirks." He was a character, alright. Moved down to Florida after his wife passed. Bought a little condo near the beach. I went down to visit him once, but the humidity was just awful. Felt like you were breathing through a wet towel the whole time. I don't know how people live down there year-round. Plus, the bugs are the size of small birds. 

But, uh... what was I saying? Right, avoiding thinking about it. Yeah, I just try to stay away from the garage and the center. Keep my mind off it if I can.

Beautiful car, that was. A '64 Fairlane. Cherry red. I used to wash it every Sunday morning before church. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he had a Chevrolet, and he was always trying to race me down to the stop sign. Henderson was a funny guy, always wore these bright yellow suspenders. Said they kept his pants up better than a belt because he didn't have no waist. His wife made the best peach cobbler, though. She'd bring a whole pan over on the Fourth of July, fresh peaches right off the tree in their backyard. You just can't buy peaches like that at the supermarket anymore. They pick 'em too green now, and they taste like wet cardboard by the time they get to the produce aisle. It's a real shame, if you ask me. \n\nBut, uh... what was it you asked me? Oh, right, avoiding things. Yeah, I just find excuses not to go out to the garage or the center. Just easier to stay on the porch.\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\": \"determine the presence of hyperarousal\", \"question\": \"Do you often feel \"on edge\" or easily startled?\"}\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I do. If a door bangs or someone comes up behind me, I jump hard. My chest just keeps thumping for a while, even after I know it's nothing. \n\nCourse, doors banging reminds me of the screen door we had on the old house back in, oh, must have been '68. The spring on that thing was so tight, it would slam shut like a gunshot every time one of the kids ran out to the yard. I tried to fix it once with some wire I had laying around, but it just made it worse. My oldest boy, Tommy, he used to let it slam on purpose just to get a rise out of the dog. We had this old hound named Buster. Best hunting dog I ever saw, but he was terrified of thunderstorms. If he even smelled rain coming, he'd crawl right under the porch and wouldn't come out for two days. Tommy loved that dog, though. Fed him scraps from the table when his mother wasn't looking. She always made this incredible meatloaf on Tuesdays. Had a special glaze she put on top with brown sugar and ketchup. I tried to make it myself a few years back, but it just didn't taste the same. I think they changed the ketchup recipe. Everything tastes different now, all high-fructose corn syrup and preservatives. \n\nBut, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, being on edge. Yeah, I jump pretty easy these days. My heart just races.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about what happened?\n\nPatient: Well, I try to. I mean, like I said before, I keep finding reasons not to go down to the senior center, and I sure don't go out to work around the garage much anymore. Especially not near the tools or that spot where I used to keep the ladder. If I'm not around it, maybe I won't think about it so much. \n\nCourse, thinking about the garage makes me think of my old neighbor, Mr. Henderson. I mentioned him earlier, the one with the yellow suspenders. He actually helped me build some shelves out there back in... oh, must have been '92. We used this heavy oak plywood. Real sturdy stuff. Not like that particle board garbage they sell down at the big box stores now. You put a heavy toolbox on that particle board and it just bows right in the middle. Henderson had this old circular saw that weighed about twenty pounds. Sounded like an airplane taking off when you fired it up. He wouldn't let anyone else use it. Said you had to know its \"quirks.\" He was a character, alright. Moved down to Florida after his wife passed. Bought a little condo near the beach. I went down to visit him once, but the humidity was just awful. Felt like you were breathing through a wet towel the whole time. I don't know how people live down there year-round. Plus, the bugs are the size of small birds. \n\nBut, uh... what was I saying? Right, avoiding thinking about it. Yeah, I just try to stay away from the garage and the center. Keep my mind off it if I can.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever have memories of what happened that feel like they are intruding into your mind without you wanting to think about them?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, I do. Sometimes when I hear metal scrape or... or see a ladder leaning in somebody's yard, the whole thing just jumps right back in my head. Like I'm right back under there again, just waiting for help. 

Course, speaking of ladders, I used to have this old wooden extension ladder. Heavy as all get-out. Had to be careful with it, though, because wooden ladders get brittle if you leave 'em out in the sun too long. My father gave it to me when I bought my first house. He was a carpenter by trade. Built cabinets mostly. Beautiful work, all dovetail joints and solid cherry wood. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. Now everything's just glued together with that cheap laminate over top. It peels right off the second it gets a little damp. He used to make these little wooden toys for the grandkids, too. Cars and trains, mostly. Painted 'em with this lead-free enamel he ordered special from a catalogue. My youngest, Sarah, she had this little wooden duck on a string she'd pull around the house all day. Quack, quack, quack. Drove her mother crazy. We ended up hiding it on top of the refrigerator for a week just to get some peace and quiet. 

But, uh... what was the question? Oh, right, the memories. Yeah, they just pop in my head when I see a ladder or hear metal scraping. It's like I'm right back there.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I suppose feeling things just... it gets tied up with it all. Like I said before, I keep finding reasons not to go to the senior center, or work around the garage. Especially near the tools and that spot where I used to keep the ladder. If I don't go there, maybe I don't have to feel that... that panic again. 

Course, talking about feelings makes me think of my sister, Martha. She was always the emotional one in the family. Cried at everything. Commercials, greeting cards, you name it. She had this collection of porcelain figurines. Little shepherds and sheep and whatnot. Dust collectors, I called 'em. But she loved them. Kept them in this glass curio cabinet she got at an estate sale back in '85. It had this little light at the top that would shine down on them. She spent hours arranging them just right. She had this one figurine of a little boy holding a puppy, and the puppy's ear was chipped off. She told me she glued it back on with some special epoxy she got from a hobby shop, but I could always see the crack. She was so proud of that fix, though. She used to bake these incredible snickerdoodle cookies, too. Rolled 'em in cinnamon and sugar. The whole house would smell like a bakery when she was visiting. I tried to find a candle that smelled like that once, but it just smelled like burnt plastic and headaches. 

But, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, avoiding feelings. Yeah, I just try to stay away from the garage and the center, you know? Keep away from the spots that bring it all up.

She told me she glued it back on with some special epoxy she got from a hobby shop, but I could always see the crack. She was so proud of that fix, though. She used to bake these incredible snickerdoodle cookies, too. Rolled 'em in cinnamon and sugar. The whole house would smell like a bakery when she was visiting. I tried to find a candle that smelled like that once, but it just smelled like burnt plastic and headaches. \n\nBut, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, avoiding feelings. Yeah, I just try to stay away from the garage and the center, you know? Keep away from the spots that bring it all up.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself, when you're reminded of what happened?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 74 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I can't say I've ever felt like that. Outside my body? No, I'm always right there in it, unfortunately. 

Course, talking about bodies makes me think of my old doctor, Dr. Aris. He was our family physician for, oh, must have been forty years. He had this old leather doctor's bag he carried everywhere. Real thick, dark brown leather. The brass clasp on it was worn smooth from him opening and closing it so many times. He used to do house calls, you know. You don't see that anymore. Now you have to sit in a waiting room for two hours reading magazines from three years ago just to get five minutes with a doctor who barely looks up from his computer screen. Dr. Aris, he'd sit at your kitchen table, have a cup of coffee, and really listen to you. He drove this big boat of a car, an old Lincoln Continental. Powder blue. Thing handled like a barge, but he loved it. Kept it immaculate, too. He'd wash it every Sunday morning in his driveway, wearing these old rubber galoshes. Used a real chamois cloth to dry it. Said the synthetic ones left streaks. He had this little terrier, too. Scruffy little thing named Barnaby. Used to ride shotgun in the Lincoln with his head out the window, ears flapping in the wind. 

But, uh... what was I saying? Right, outside my body. No, I don't get anything like that. Just feel like myself, for better or worse.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I wouldn't say I avoid things I enjoy, exactly. I just... I don't know, I guess I haven't been doing much of anything lately. Just sitting around the house. 

Course, speaking of sitting around, I used to have this old recliner. Best chair I ever owned. It was upholstered in this hideous green plaid fabric, looked like something out of a hunting lodge from the seventies. My wife hated it. Said it clashed with the curtains. But it was so comfortable. The springs were perfectly broken in, and it had this wooden lever on the side that you'd pull to make the footrest pop up. It made this satisfying *clunk* sound every time. I'd sit in that chair on Sunday afternoons, listening to the baseball game on the radio. AM radio, you know, with all the static. You had to fiddle with the dial just right to get a clear signal. I had this old Zenith radio, wood casing, real heavy. The dial lit up with this warm orange glow. I remember listening to the '75 World Series on that thing. Reds and Red Sox. What a series that was. Game six, Carlton Fisk waving that home run fair. You don't see baseball like that anymore. Now it's all analytics and launch angles. They don't even let the pitchers finish a game. Just bring in a new guy every inning. It's exhausting to watch. 

But, uh... what was the question? Oh, right, avoiding things I enjoy. No, I don't think I avoid them because they make me upset. I just... I don't have the energy for much these days.

  {
    "role": "system",

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, mostly I'm here because I don't sleep right anymore. I just lie there a long time in the dark. It reminds me of back when I was working on the cars, you know, my buddy Earl and I would be up half the night trying to get an engine to turn over. Earl always had this old radio playing, static mostly... anyway, now I finally drift off and then I wake up from bad dreams or just little noises outside, like a dog barking three streets over, and I can't seem to settle back down. I tried drinking a glass of warm milk, my mother used to swear by that when I was a boy, but it just gave me heartburn.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to concentrate or feel fatigued during the day?\n\nPatient: Concentrate? No, I wouldn't say that. My mind is still pretty sharp, and I don't really get tired during the day, neither. I mostly just sit out on the porch or watch the television. \n\nCourse, the TV's been acting up lately. The picture gets all fuzzy on the local channel. Reminds me of this big heavy Zenith set my wife and I bought back in, oh, must've been '78. Cost me a week's pay, and we still had to fiddle with the rabbit ears for an hour just to watch the evening news. We loved Walter Cronkite. They just don't make newsmen like him anymore, do they? Now everybody on the screen is just yelling at each other about something, makes my head spin just listening to 'em. \n\nBut, uh... what were we... oh, no, I'm not fatigued or nothing like that. Just regular, I guess.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned waking up from bad dreams, can you tell me more about those dreams?\n\nPatient: Well, the dreams are mostly about when it happened. When it all came down. I wake up and for a second I think I'm still stuck. But it happens when I'm awake, too. Sometimes if I just hear a piece of metal scrape, or if I'm out walking and see a ladder leaning in somebody's yard, the whole thing just jumps back in my head. It's like I'm right back under there again waiting for help.\n\nCourse, ladders remind me of this old aluminum extension ladder I used to have. Bought it at the Sears and Roebuck over in the valley. Must have been the mid-eighties. Sears had a real nice hardware department back then. You could walk in and smell the rubber tires and the machine oil. I bought a Craftsman drill the same day. Heavy thing, solid metal casing. Not like the cheap plastic junk they sell today. My grandson ordered some tools off the computer last month, and I told him he was just throwing his money away on that plastic. He didn't listen, though. Nobody listens to the old guys anymore. \n\nBut, uh... what was I saying? Right, the dreams. Yeah, they just put me right back there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities because they remind you of what happened?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do. I just... I keep finding reasons not to go down to the senior center these days. And I sure don't go out to work around the garage much anymore, especially not near the tools or that spot where I used to keep that ladder. Just don't want to be around it. \n\nCourse, the garage is a mess anyway. I got boxes of old oil filters and spark plugs out there from when I used to fix up that old Ford I had. Beautiful car, that was. A '64 Fairlane. Cherry red. I used to wash it every Sunday morning before church. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he had a Chevrolet, and he was always trying to race me down to the stop sign. Henderson was a funny guy, always wore these bright yellow suspenders. Said they kept his pants up better than a belt because he didn't have no waist. His wife made the best peach cobbler, though. She'd bring a whole pan over on the Fourth of July, fresh peaches right off the tree in their backyard. You just can't buy peaches like that at the supermarket anymore. They pick 'em too green now, and they taste like wet cardboard by the time they get to the produce aisle. It's a real shame, if you ask me. \n\nBut, uh... what was it you asked me? Oh, right, avoiding things. Yeah, I just find excuses not to go out to the garage or the center. Just easier to stay on the porch.\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\": \"determine the presence of hyperarousal\", \"question\": \"Do you often feel \"on edge\" or easily startled?\"}\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I do. If a door bangs or someone comes up behind me, I jump hard. My chest just keeps thumping for a while, even after I know it's nothing. \n\nCourse, doors banging reminds me of the screen door we had on the old house back in, oh, must have been '68. The spring on that thing was so tight, it would slam shut like a gunshot every time one of the kids ran out to the yard. I tried to fix it once with some wire I had laying around, but it just made it worse. My oldest boy, Tommy, he used to let it slam on purpose just to get a rise out of the dog. We had this old hound named Buster. Best hunting dog I ever saw, but he was terrified of thunderstorms. If he even smelled rain coming, he'd crawl right under the porch and wouldn't come out for two days. Tommy loved that dog, though. Fed him scraps from the table when his mother wasn't looking. She always made this incredible meatloaf on Tuesdays. Had a special glaze she put on top with brown sugar and ketchup. I tried to make it myself a few years back, but it just didn't taste the same. I think they changed the ketchup recipe. Everything tastes different now, all high-fructose corn syrup and preservatives. \n\nBut, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, being on edge. Yeah, I jump pretty easy these days. My heart just races.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about what happened?\n\nPatient: Well, I try to. I mean, like I said before, I keep finding reasons not to go down to the senior center, and I sure don't go out to work around the garage much anymore. Especially not near the tools or that spot where I used to keep the ladder. If I'm not around it, maybe I won't think about it so much. \n\nCourse, thinking about the garage makes me think of my old neighbor, Mr. Henderson. I mentioned him earlier, the one with the yellow suspenders. He actually helped me build some shelves out there back in... oh, must have been '92. We used this heavy oak plywood. Real sturdy stuff. Not like that particle board garbage they sell down at the big box stores now. You put a heavy toolbox on that particle board and it just bows right in the middle. Henderson had this old circular saw that weighed about twenty pounds. Sounded like an airplane taking off when you fired it up. He wouldn't let anyone else use it. Said you had to know its \"quirks.\" He was a character, alright. Moved down to Florida after his wife passed. Bought a little condo near the beach. I went down to visit him once, but the humidity was just awful. Felt like you were breathing through a wet towel the whole time. I don't know how people live down there year-round. Plus, the bugs are the size of small birds. \n\nBut, uh... what was I saying? Right, avoiding thinking about it. Yeah, I just try to stay away from the garage and the center. Keep my mind off it if I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories of what happened that feel like they are intruding into your mind without you wanting to think about them?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I do. He was our family physician for, oh, must have been forty years. He had this old leather doctor's bag he carried everywhere. Real thick, dark brown leather. The brass clasp on it was worn smooth from him opening and closing it so many times. He used to do house calls, you know. You don't see that anymore. Now you have to sit in a waiting room for two hours reading magazines from three years ago just to get five minutes with a doctor who barely looks up from his computer screen. Dr. Aris, he'd sit at your kitchen table, have a cup of coffee, and really listen to you. He drove this big boat of a car, an old Lincoln Continental. Powder blue. Thing handled like a barge, but he loved it. Kept it immaculate, too. He'd wash it every Sunday morning in his driveway, wearing these old rubber galoshes. Used a real chamois cloth to dry it. Said the synthetic ones left streaks. He had this little terrier, too. Scruffy little thing named Barnaby. Used to ride shotgun in the Lincoln with his head out the window, ears flapping in the wind. \n\nBut, uh... what was I saying? Right, outside my body. No, I don't get anything like that. Just feel like myself, for better or worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid doing things that you used to enjoy because they make you feel anxious or upset?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I avoid things I enjoy, exactly. I just... I don't know, I guess I haven't been doing much of anything lately. Just sitting around the house. \n\nCourse, speaking of sitting around, I used to have this old recliner. Best chair I ever owned. It was upholstered in this hideous green plaid fabric, looked like something out of a hunting lodge from the seventies. My wife hated it. Said it clashed with the curtains. But it was so comfortable. The springs were perfectly broken in, and it had this wooden lever on the side that you'd pull to make the footrest pop up. It made this satisfying *clunk* sound every time. I'd sit in that chair on Sunday afternoons, listening to the baseball game on the radio. AM radio, you know, with all the static. You had to fiddle with the dial just right to get a clear signal. I had this old Zenith radio, wood casing, real heavy. The dial lit up with this warm orange glow. I remember listening to the '75 World Series on that thing. Reds and Red Sox. What a series that was. Game six, Carlton Fisk waving that home run fair. You don't see baseball like that anymore. Now it's all analytics and launch angles. They don't even let the pitchers finish a game. Just bring in a new guy every inning. It's exhausting to watch. \n\nBut, uh... what was the question? Oh, right, avoiding things I enjoy. No, I don't think I avoid them because they make me upset. I just... I don't have the energy for much these days.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel like you're reliving the moment when it happened, like it's happening all over again?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 74 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Intrusive_Symptoms\nmanifestation: \"Sometimes when I hear metal scrape or see a ladder leaning in somebody's yard, the whole thing jumps back in my head like I'm under there again waiting for help.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yeah, sometimes I do. It's... it's like if I hear metal scraping, or if I just see a ladder leaning up against somebody's house, the whole thing just jumps right back into my head. It feels like I'm pinned under there all over again, just waiting for somebody to come help me. 

Course, talking about ladders reminds me of my neighbor, Frank. He had this aluminum extension ladder, must have been forty feet long. Heaviest thing you ever tried to lift. He used it to clean his gutters every fall. He had these big oak trees in his yard, dropped leaves the size of dinner plates. He'd be up there for hours, scooping out this black, rotting muck. He always wore these bright yellow rubber gloves, looked like he was about to do surgery or wash a sink full of dishes. Frank was a character. He used to grow these prize-winning tomatoes. Beefsteaks, mostly. He had this secret fertilizer mix he swore by. Something to do with eggshells and coffee grounds. He'd save them up in this old Folgers can on his back porch. The smell was awful, especially in August when the sun hit it just right. But I'll tell you, those tomatoes were something else. Slice one up, put a little salt and pepper on it, maybe some mayonnaise on white bread... best sandwich you'd ever eat. He tried to grow cucumbers once, but the beetles got to 'em. Ate the leaves right down to the stems. 

But, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, reliving it. Yeah, the metal scraping and the ladders... it just puts me right back under there.

Yeah, they just put me right back there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities because they remind you of what happened?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do. I just... My chest just keeps thumping for a while, even after I know it's nothing. \n\nCourse, doors banging reminds me of the screen door we had on the old house back in, oh, must have been '68. The spring on that thing was so tight, it would slam shut like a gunshot every time one of the kids ran out to the yard. I tried to fix it once with some wire I had laying around, but it just made it worse. My oldest boy, Tommy, he used to let it slam on purpose just to get a rise out of the dog. We had this old hound named Buster. Best hunting dog I ever saw, but he was terrified of thunderstorms. If he even smelled rain coming, he'd crawl right under the porch and wouldn't come out for two days. Tommy loved that dog, though. Fed him scraps from the table when his mother wasn't looking. She always made this incredible meatloaf on Tuesdays. Had a special glaze she put on top with brown sugar and ketchup. I tried to make it myself a few years back, but it just didn't taste the same. I think they changed the ketchup recipe. Everything tastes different now, all high-fructose corn syrup and preservatives. \n\nBut, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, being on edge. Now everything's just glued together with that cheap laminate over top. It peels right off the second it gets a little damp. He used to make these little wooden toys for the grandkids, too. Cars and trains, mostly. Painted 'em with this lead-free enamel he ordered special from a catalogue. My youngest, Sarah, she had this little wooden duck on a string she'd pull around the house all day. Quack, quack, quack. Drove her mother crazy. We ended up hiding it on top of the refrigerator for a week just to get some peace and quiet. \n\nBut, uh... what was the question? Oh, right, the memories. Keep my mind off it if I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories of what happened that feel like they are intruding into your mind without you wanting to think about them?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I do. Sometimes when I hear metal scrape or... or see a ladder leaning in somebody's yard, the whole thing just jumps right back in my head. Like I'm right back under there again, just waiting for help. \n\nCourse, speaking of ladders, I used to have this old wooden extension ladder. Heavy as all get-out. Had to be careful with it, though, because wooden ladders get brittle if you leave 'em out in the sun too long. My father gave it to me when I bought my first house. He was a carpenter by trade. Built cabinets mostly. Beautiful work, all dovetail joints and solid cherry wood. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. Now everything's just glued together with that cheap laminate over top. It peels right off the second it gets a little damp. He used to make these little wooden toys for the grandkids, too. Cars and trains, mostly. Painted 'em with this lead-free enamel he ordered special from a catalogue. My youngest, Sarah, she had this little wooden duck on a string she'd pull around the house all day. Quack, quack, quack. Drove her mother crazy. We ended up hiding it on top of the refrigerator for a week just to get some peace and quiet. \n\nBut, uh... what was the question? Oh, right, the memories. Yeah, they just pop in my head when I see a ladder or hear metal scraping. It's like I'm right back there.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid feeling certain emotions because they remind you of what happened?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose feeling things just... it gets tied up with it all. Like I said before, I keep finding reasons not to go to the senior center, or work around the garage. Especially near the tools and that spot where I used to keep the ladder. If I don't go there, maybe I don't have to feel that... that panic again. \n\nCourse, talking about feelings makes me think of my sister, Martha. She was always the emotional one in the family. Cried at everything. Commercials, greeting cards, you name it. She had this collection of porcelain figurines. Little shepherds and sheep and whatnot. Dust collectors, I called 'em. But she loved them. Kept them in this glass curio cabinet she got at an estate sale back in '85. It had this little light at the top that would shine down on them. She spent hours arranging them just right. She had this one figurine of a little boy holding a puppy, and the puppy's ear was chipped off. She told me she glued it back on with some special epoxy she got from a hobby shop, but I could always see the crack. She was so proud of that fix, though. She used to bake these incredible snickerdoodle cookies, too. Rolled 'em in cinnamon and sugar. The whole house would smell like a bakery when she was visiting. I tried to find a candle that smelled like that once, but it just smelled like burnt plastic and headaches. \n\nBut, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, avoiding feelings. Yeah, I just try to stay away from the garage and the center, you know? Keep away from the spots that bring it all up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself, when you're reminded of what happened?\n\nPatient: No, I can't say I've ever felt like that. Outside my body? No, I'm always right there in it, unfortunately. \n\nCourse, talking about bodies makes me think of my old doctor, Dr. Aris. He was our family physician for, oh, must have been forty years. He had this old leather doctor's bag he carried everywhere. Real thick, dark brown leather. The brass clasp on it was worn smooth from him opening and closing it so many times. He used to do house calls, you know. You don't see that anymore. Now you have to sit in a waiting room for two hours reading magazines from three years ago just to get five minutes with a doctor who barely looks up from his computer screen. Dr. Aris, he'd sit at your kitchen table, have a cup of coffee, and really listen to you. He drove this big boat of a car, an old Lincoln Continental. Powder blue. Thing handled like a barge, but he loved it. Kept it immaculate, too. He'd wash it every Sunday morning in his driveway, wearing these old rubber galoshes. Used a real chamois cloth to dry it. Said the synthetic ones left streaks. He had this little terrier, too. Scruffy little thing named Barnaby. Used to ride shotgun in the Lincoln with his head out the window, ears flapping in the wind. \n\nBut, uh... what was I saying? Right, outside my body. No, I don't get anything like that. Just feel like myself, for better or worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid doing things that you used to enjoy because they make you feel anxious or upset?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I avoid things I enjoy, exactly. I just... I don't know, I guess I haven't been doing much of anything lately. Just sitting around the house. \n\nCourse, speaking of sitting around, I used to have this old recliner. Best chair I ever owned. It was upholstered in this hideous green plaid fabric, looked like something out of a hunting lodge from the seventies. My wife hated it. Said it clashed with the curtains. But it was so comfortable. The springs were perfectly broken in, and it had this wooden lever on the side that you'd pull to make the footrest pop up. It made this satisfying *clunk* sound every time. I'd sit in that chair on Sunday afternoons, listening to the baseball game on the radio. AM radio, you know, with all the static. You had to fiddle with the dial just right to get a clear signal. I had this old Zenith radio, wood casing, real heavy. The dial lit up with this warm orange glow. I remember listening to the '75 World Series on that thing. Reds and Red Sox. What a series that was. Game six, Carlton Fisk waving that home run fair. You don't see baseball like that anymore. Now it's all analytics and launch angles. They don't even let the pitchers finish a game. Just bring in a new guy every inning. It's exhausting to watch. \n\nBut, uh... what was the question? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Numb? No, I can't say I do. I don't feel detached or anything like that. I'm just right here. 

Course, talking about feeling numb makes me think of my old dentist, Dr. Higgins. Went to him to get a back molar pulled in, oh, must've been '82. He gave me a shot of that Novocaine with a needle that looked like it belonged on an industrial sewing machine. My whole jaw was numb for two solid days. I couldn't even eat my wife's meatloaf. She made the best meatloaf, you know. Put this thick ketchup glaze right on the top, baked it in one of those heavy glass Pyrex dishes. We had this old gas stove back then, the kind you had to light with a match if the pilot light went out. Always smelled a little bit like sulfur in the kitchen right before the burner caught. She'd serve that meatloaf with mashed potatoes. Not the boxed flake kind, the real ones. She had this old metal hand masher with a red wooden handle. Most of the red paint had chipped off over the years, just from her washing it in the soapy water. She'd put a whole stick of butter in those potatoes, splash of whole milk. You don't see people cooking like that much anymore. Everybody's worried about their cholesterol now, or they're just eating out of those plastic microwave trays. 

But, uh... what was the question again? Oh, right, feeling numb about the accident. No, I don't get that way. I just feel regular, mostly.

Reminds me of this big heavy Zenith set my wife and I bought back in, oh, must've been '78. When it all came down. I wake up and for a second I think I'm still stuck. But it happens when I'm awake, too. Sometimes if I just hear a piece of metal scrape, or if I'm out walking and see a ladder leaning in somebody's yard, the whole thing just jumps back in my head. It's like I'm right back under there again waiting for help.\n\nCourse, ladders remind me of this old aluminum extension ladder I used to have. Bought it at the Sears and Roebuck over in the valley. Must have been the mid-eighties. Sears had a real nice hardware department back then. You could walk in and smell the rubber tires and the machine oil. I bought a Craftsman drill the same day. Heavy thing, solid metal casing. Not like the cheap plastic junk they sell today. My grandson ordered some tools off the computer last month, and I told him he was just throwing his money away on that plastic. He didn't listen, though. Nobody listens to the old guys anymore. \n\nBut, uh... what was I saying? Right, the dreams. Yeah, they just put me right back there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities because they remind you of what happened?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do. I just... I keep finding reasons not to go down to the senior center these days. And I sure don't go out to work around the garage much anymore, especially not near the tools or that spot where I used to keep that ladder. Just don't want to be around it. \n\nCourse, the garage is a mess anyway. I got boxes of old oil filters and spark plugs out there from when I used to fix up that old Ford I had. Beautiful car, that was. A '64 Fairlane. Cherry red. I used to wash it every Sunday morning before church. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he had a Chevrolet, and he was always trying to race me down to the stop sign. Henderson was a funny guy, always wore these bright yellow suspenders. Said they kept his pants up better than a belt because he didn't have no waist. His wife made the best peach cobbler, though. She'd bring a whole pan over on the Fourth of July, fresh peaches right off the tree in their backyard. You just can't buy peaches like that at the supermarket anymore. They pick 'em too green now, and they taste like wet cardboard by the time they get to the produce aisle. It's a real shame, if you ask me. \n\nBut, uh... what was it you asked me? Oh, right, avoiding things. Yeah, I just find excuses not to go out to the garage or the center. Just easier to stay on the porch.\n\nDoctor: {\"intent\": \"determine the presence of hyperarousal\", \"question\": \"Do you often feel \"on edge\" or easily startled?\"}\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I guess I do. If a door bangs or someone comes up behind me, I jump hard. My chest just keeps thumping for a while, even after I know it's nothing. \n\nCourse, doors banging reminds me of the screen door we had on the old house back in, oh, must have been '68. The spring on that thing was so tight, it would slam shut like a gunshot every time one of the kids ran out to the yard. I tried to fix it once with some wire I had laying around, but it just made it worse. My oldest boy, Tommy, he used to let it slam on purpose just to get a rise out of the dog. We had this old hound named Buster. Best hunting dog I ever saw, but he was terrified of thunderstorms. If he even smelled rain coming, he'd crawl right under the porch and wouldn't come out for two days. Tommy loved that dog, though. Fed him scraps from the table when his mother wasn't looking. She always made this incredible meatloaf on Tuesdays. Had a special glaze she put on top with brown sugar and ketchup. I tried to make it myself a few years back, but it just didn't taste the same. I think they changed the ketchup recipe. Everything tastes different now, all high-fructose corn syrup and preservatives. \n\nBut, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, being on edge. Yeah, I jump pretty easy these days. My heart just races.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about what happened?\n\nPatient: Well, I try to. I mean, like I said before, I keep finding reasons not to go down to the senior center, and I sure don't go out to work around the garage much anymore. Especially not near the tools or that spot where I used to keep the ladder. If I'm not around it, maybe I won't think about it so much. \n\nCourse, thinking about the garage makes me think of my old neighbor, Mr. Henderson. I mentioned him earlier, the one with the yellow suspenders. He actually helped me build some shelves out there back in... oh, must have been '92. We used this heavy oak plywood. Real sturdy stuff. Not like that particle board garbage they sell down at the big box stores now. You put a heavy toolbox on that particle board and it just bows right in the middle. Henderson had this old circular saw that weighed about twenty pounds. Sounded like an airplane taking off when you fired it up. He wouldn't let anyone else use it. Said you had to know its \"quirks.\" He was a character, alright. Moved down to Florida after his wife passed. Bought a little condo near the beach. I went down to visit him once, but the humidity was just awful. Felt like you were breathing through a wet towel the whole time. I don't know how people live down there year-round. Plus, the bugs are the size of small birds. \n\nBut, uh... what was I saying? Right, avoiding thinking about it. Yeah, I just try to stay away from the garage and the center. Keep my mind off it if I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories of what happened that feel like they are intruding into your mind without you wanting to think about them?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I do. Sometimes when I hear metal scrape or... or see a ladder leaning in somebody's yard, the whole thing just jumps right back in my head. Like I'm right back under there again, just waiting for help. \n\nCourse, speaking of ladders, I used to have this old wooden extension ladder. Heavy as all get-out. Had to be careful with it, though, because wooden ladders get brittle if you leave 'em out in the sun too long. My father gave it to me when I bought my first house. He was a carpenter by trade. Built cabinets mostly. Beautiful work, all dovetail joints and solid cherry wood. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. Now everything's just glued together with that cheap laminate over top. It peels right off the second it gets a little damp. He used to make these little wooden toys for the grandkids, too. Cars and trains, mostly. Painted 'em with this lead-free enamel he ordered special from a catalogue. My youngest, Sarah, she had this little wooden duck on a string she'd pull around the house all day. Quack, quack, quack. Drove her mother crazy. We ended up hiding it on top of the refrigerator for a week just to get some peace and quiet. \n\nBut, uh... what was the question? Oh, right, the memories. Yeah, they just pop in my head when I see a ladder or hear metal scraping. It's like I'm right back there.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid feeling certain emotions because they remind you of what happened?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose feeling things just... it gets tied up with it all. Like I said before, I keep finding reasons not to go to the senior center, or work around the garage. Especially near the tools and that spot where I used to keep the ladder. If I don't go there, maybe I don't have to feel that... that panic again. \n\nCourse, talking about feelings makes me think of my sister, Martha. She was always the emotional one in the family. Cried at everything. Commercials, greeting cards, you name it. She had this collection of porcelain figurines. Little shepherds and sheep and whatnot. Dust collectors, I called 'em. But she loved them. Kept them in this glass curio cabinet she got at an estate sale back in '85. It had this little light at the top that would shine down on them. She spent hours arranging them just right. She had this one figurine of a little boy holding a puppy, and the puppy's ear was chipped off. She told me she glued it back on with some special epoxy she got from a hobby shop, but I could always see the crack. She was so proud of that fix, though. She used to bake these incredible snickerdoodle cookies, too. Rolled 'em in cinnamon and sugar. The whole house would smell like a bakery when she was visiting. I tried to find a candle that smelled like that once, but it just smelled like burnt plastic and headaches. \n\nBut, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, avoiding feelings. Yeah, I just try to stay away from the garage and the center, you know? Keep away from the spots that bring it all up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself, when you're reminded of what happened?\n\nPatient: No, I can't say I've ever felt like that. Outside my body? No, I'm always right there in it, unfortunately. \n\nCourse, talking about bodies makes me think of my old doctor, Dr. Aris. He was our family physician for, oh, must have been forty years. He had this old leather doctor's bag he carried everywhere. Real thick, dark brown leather. The brass clasp on it was worn smooth from him opening and closing it so many times. He used to do house calls, you know. You don't see that anymore. Now you have to sit in a waiting room for two hours reading magazines from three years ago just to get five minutes with a doctor who barely looks up from his computer screen. Dr. Aris, he'd sit at your kitchen table, have a cup of coffee, and really listen to you. He drove this big boat of a car, an old Lincoln Continental. Powder blue. Thing handled like a barge, but he loved it. Kept it immaculate, too. He'd wash it every Sunday morning in his driveway, wearing these old rubber galoshes. Used a real chamois cloth to dry it. Said the synthetic ones left streaks. He had this little terrier, too. Scruffy little thing named Barnaby. Used to ride shotgun in the Lincoln with his head out the window, ears flapping in the wind. \n\nBut, uh... what was I saying? Right, outside my body. No, I don't get anything like that. Just feel like myself, for better or worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid doing things that you used to enjoy because they make you feel anxious or upset?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I avoid things I enjoy, exactly. I just... I don't know, I guess I haven't been doing much of anything lately. Just sitting around the house. \n\nCourse, speaking of sitting around, I used to have this old recliner. Best chair I ever owned. It was upholstered in this hideous green plaid fabric, looked like something out of a hunting lodge from the seventies. My wife hated it. Said it clashed with the curtains. But it was so comfortable. The springs were perfectly broken in, and it had this wooden lever on the side that you'd pull to make the footrest pop up. It made this satisfying *clunk* sound every time. I'd sit in that chair on Sunday afternoons, listening to the baseball game on the radio. AM radio, you know, with all the static. You had to fiddle with the dial just right to get a clear signal. I had this old Zenith radio, wood casing, real heavy. The dial lit up with this warm orange glow. I remember listening to the '75 World Series on that thing. Reds and Red Sox. What a series that was. Game six, Carlton Fisk waving that home run fair. You don't see baseball like that anymore. Now it's all analytics and launch angles. They don't even let the pitchers finish a game. Just bring in a new guy every inning. It's exhausting to watch. \n\nBut, uh... what was the question? Oh, right, avoiding things I enjoy. No, I don't think I avoid them because they make me upset. I just... I don't have the energy for much these days.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're reliving the moment when it happened, like it's happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, sometimes I do. It's... it's like if I hear metal scraping, or if I just see a ladder leaning up against somebody's house, the whole thing just jumps right back into my head. It feels like I'm pinned under there all over again, just waiting for somebody to come help me. \n\nCourse, talking about ladders reminds me of my neighbor, Frank. He had this aluminum extension ladder, must have been forty feet long. Heaviest thing you ever tried to lift. He used it to clean his gutters every fall. He had these big oak trees in his yard, dropped leaves the size of dinner plates. He'd be up there for hours, scooping out this black, rotting muck. He always wore these bright yellow rubber gloves, looked like he was about to do surgery or wash a sink full of dishes. Frank was a character. He used to grow these prize-winning tomatoes. Beefsteaks, mostly. He had this secret fertilizer mix he swore by. Something to do with eggshells and coffee grounds. He'd save them up in this old Folgers can on his back porch. The smell was awful, especially in August when the sun hit it just right. But I'll tell you, those tomatoes were something else. Slice one up, put a little salt and pepper on it, maybe some mayonnaise on white bread... best sandwich you'd ever eat. He tried to grow cucumbers once, but the beetles got to 'em. Ate the leaves right down to the stems. \n\nBut, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, reliving it. Yeah, the metal scraping and the ladders... it just puts me right back under there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel numb or detached from the world around you when reminded of what happened?\n\nPatient: Numb? No, I can't say I do. I don't feel detached or anything like that. I'm just right here. \n\nCourse, talking about feeling numb makes me think of my old dentist, Dr. Higgins. Went to him to get a back molar pulled in, oh, must've been '82. He gave me a shot of that Novocaine with a needle that looked like it belonged on an industrial sewing machine. My whole jaw was numb for two solid days. I couldn't even eat my wife's meatloaf. She made the best meatloaf, you know. Put this thick ketchup glaze right on the top, baked it in one of those heavy glass Pyrex dishes. We had this old gas stove back then, the kind you had to light with a match if the pilot light went out. Always smelled a little bit like sulfur in the kitchen right before the burner caught. She'd serve that meatloaf with mashed potatoes. Not the boxed flake kind, the real ones. She had this old metal hand masher with a red wooden handle. Most of the red paint had chipped off over the years, just from her washing it in the soapy water. She'd put a whole stick of butter in those potatoes, splash of whole milk. You don't see people cooking like that much anymore. Everybody's worried about their cholesterol now, or they're just eating out of those plastic microwave trays. \n\nBut, uh... what was the question again? Oh, right, feeling numb about the accident. No, I don't get that way. I just feel regular, mostly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I don't know if it's avoiding the people exactly, but I do keep finding reasons not to go down to the senior center. And I haven't been wanting to work around the garage much either. 

Course, speaking of the garage, I used to spend all day out there at my workbench. Built it myself out of some scrap two-by-fours and a thick piece of plywood. Had this heavy iron vise bolted to the front corner. Painted blue, though most of the paint's chipped off now. I used to clamp my lawnmower blades in there to sharpen 'em. I had this old metal file, the wooden handle was worn perfectly smooth right where my thumb sat. You had to get the angle just right, push away from you, never pull back. My dad taught me that. He worked as a mechanic down at the old Ford dealership. He'd come home every night smelling like motor oil and Lava soap. You remember Lava soap? Had that pumice in it, felt like washing your hands with a handful of sand, but it was the only thing that cut the grease. He'd stand at the kitchen sink scrubbing for ten minutes before my mother would let him sit at the table. We had this Formica table, yellow with little gray flecks in it. Chrome legs. My mother would wipe it down with a damp rag after every meal, catching all the crumbs in her hand so they wouldn't hit the linoleum. 

But, uh... what did you ask me? Oh, right, avoiding folks. Yeah, I just keep making up excuses to skip the senior center. And like I said, I stay out of the garage, especially over near the tools and that spot where I used to keep the ladder. I just don't want to be around it.

Heavy thing, solid metal casing. Not like the cheap plastic junk they sell today. Nobody listens to the old guys anymore. \n\nBut, uh... what was I saying? Right, the dreams. Yeah, they just put me right back there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities because they remind you of what happened?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I do. I just... And I sure don't go out to work around the garage much anymore, especially not near the tools or that spot where I used to keep that ladder. Beautiful car, that was. A '64 Fairlane. Cherry red. I used to wash it every Sunday morning before church. My neighbor, Mr. Henderson, he had a Chevrolet, and he was always trying to race me down to the stop sign. Henderson was a funny guy, always wore these bright yellow suspenders. Said they kept his pants up better than a belt because he didn't have no waist. His wife made the best peach cobbler, though. She'd bring a whole pan over on the Fourth of July, fresh peaches right off the tree in their backyard. You just can't buy peaches like that at the supermarket anymore. If a door bangs or someone comes up behind me, I jump hard. My chest just keeps thumping for a while, even after I know it's nothing. \n\nCourse, doors banging reminds me of the screen door we had on the old house back in, oh, must have been '68. The spring on that thing was so tight, it would slam shut like a gunshot every time one of the kids ran out to the yard. I tried to fix it once with some wire I had laying around, but it just made it worse. My oldest boy, Tommy, he used to let it slam on purpose just to get a rise out of the dog. We had this old hound named Buster. Best hunting dog I ever saw, but he was terrified of thunderstorms. If he even smelled rain coming, he'd crawl right under the porch and wouldn't come out for two days. Tommy loved that dog, though. Fed him scraps from the table when his mother wasn't looking. She always made this incredible meatloaf on Tuesdays. Had a special glaze she put on top with brown sugar and ketchup. I tried to make it myself a few years back, but it just didn't taste the same. I think they changed the ketchup recipe. Everything tastes different now, all high-fructose corn syrup and preservatives. \n\nBut, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, being on edge. Yeah, I jump pretty easy these days. My heart just races.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid thinking about what happened?\n\nPatient: Well, I try to. Especially not near the tools or that spot where I used to keep the ladder. I mentioned him earlier, the one with the yellow suspenders. He actually helped me build some shelves out there back in... oh, must have been '92. We used this heavy oak plywood. Real sturdy stuff. Not like that particle board garbage they sell down at the big box stores now. You put a heavy toolbox on that particle board and it just bows right in the middle. Henderson had this old circular saw that weighed about twenty pounds. Sounded like an airplane taking off when you fired it up. He wouldn't let anyone else use it. Said you had to know its \"quirks.\" He was a character, alright. Moved down to Florida after his wife passed. Bought a little condo near the beach. I went down to visit him once, but the humidity was just awful. Felt like you were breathing through a wet towel the whole time. I don't know how people live down there year-round. Plus, the bugs are the size of small birds. \n\nBut, uh... what was I saying? Right, avoiding thinking about it. Yeah, I just try to stay away from the garage and the center.
